In these there are no limits at all; they take no account whatever of their means and possibilities.</p> <p class="he">שישא חן בעיני ה"בריות" – "בריות בעלמא" (כלשון רבינו הזקן), שאין להם שום מעלות מלבד העובדה שנבראו ע"י הקב"ה</p> <p class="ref">B14:Q25 · Toras Menachem, Vol. 18 (5717, part 1), section 38 (line 30613)</p> <p class="en">So that he finds favour in the eyes of 'the creatures', 'mere creatures' in the Alter Rebbe's phrase, who have no virtue apart from the fact that they were created by the Holy One.</p> <p class="he">אבל אם הוא ביישן, ואעפ"כ, מכריח את עצמו לדבר עם הזולת ולפעול עליו להניח תפילין</p> <p class="ref">B14:Q26 · Toras Menachem, Vol. 52 (5728, part 2), section 33 (line 101653)</p> <p class="en">But if he is shy, and nevertheless forces himself to speak to another person and influence him to put on tefillin, [then there is real toil].</p> <p class="he">שכן, ישראל מצד טבעם הם "עזין שבאומות"… אלא שמ"ת פעל אצלם טבע הבושה</p> <p class="ref">B14:Q27 · Toras Menachem, Vol. 58 (5730, part 1) (line 104356)</p> <p class="en">For Israel by their nature are 'the boldest of the nations'... but the giving of the Torah brought about in them the nature of shame.</p> <p class="he">אשר נורמלי הוא, שלא תמיד ענין ההתרכזות במדה שוה אצלה, וכן ענין הבטחון-עצמי… לרכוש חברה וידידה אשר תוכל לדבר עמה גלוית לב</p> <p class="ref">B14:Q28 · Igros Kodesh of the Lubavitcher Rebbe, Letter ז'קלא, 28 Kislev 5720 (adjacent: a teacher's confidence) (line 84658)</p> <p class="en">It is normal that her concentration is not always at the same level, and likewise her self-confidence... [and a main thing:] to acquire a friend with whom she can speak openly.</p> <p class="he">כוונתי היתה שיהי' הענין מובן אצלו כדבעי וחקוק בזכרונו, שאז אפילו בשעה שאין זה במחשבתו בפועל בכל זה חקוקים הענינים במוח הזכרון</p> <p class="ref">B14:Q29 · Igros Kodesh of the Lubavitcher Rebbe, Letter ה'לח, 15 Teves 5717 (cross-referenced by the editors from the stammering letter) (line 61136)</p> <p class="en">My intent was that the matter [of divine providence] be properly understood by him and engraved in his memory; then even when it is not actually in his thought, these matters are engraved in the faculty of memory.</p> <p class="he">תורת אמת, שעל ידה אפשר לנצח את היצה"ר, ולהודות על הטעות (מבלי להתפעל מה יאמרו הבריות)</p> <p class="ref">B14:Q30 · Toras Menachem, Vol. 60 (5730, part 3) (line 105567)</p> <p class="en">A Torah of truth, through which one can overcome the evil inclination and admit the error (without being moved by what people will say).</p>

## Gaps

Nearly all the treatment is aimed at speech anxiety or at ridicule over religious observance. The letters give almost nothing on general social anxiety, body shame, shame after a failure or scandal, or shame from being bullied. There is no graded exposure beyond the word-swap and re-read tricks. The earlier Rebbeim's letters are silent. The Rebbe Rashab's, Tzemach Tzedek's and Alter Rebbe's letters were not searched.

## B15 · I can't get myself to start: laziness and heaviness

*I know what I need to do and I even want to, but I can't get going. I put it off, find reasons, tell myself I'm not capable or I'll start when I have time. It feels like the desire has been taken out of me, and then I feel bad about myself, which makes it heavier.*

**Coverage.** Counted planned and added probes in the Rebbe's letters (IK), Rayatz letters, Rayatz maamarim, Admur HaEmtzai, Hayom Yom and Toras Menachem. The planned probes were thin (עצלות 16, עצלות וכבדות 3, רפיון ידים 3, יגיעה עצמית 0). Added: רשלנות, אין לו חשק, עיפות/עייפות, לכשאפנה, חטוף ואכול, כבדות (almost all hits were נכבדות, meaning matchmaking). Read about 25 IK letters in context, 2 Rayatz maamar passages, 1 Hayom Yom entry and 4 Toras Menachem hits. In the Rayatz letters, עצל only matched צלחה, so nothing was usable. Admur HaEmtzai had 2 conceptual hits. About 35 tool calls.

**What the sources do.** treats it: the Rebbe's letters answer people who asked outright for a cure for laziness, and he separates laziness from fatigue. The other Rebbeim's collections here are thin.

## The reading

The Rebbe reads laziness as the animal soul's unwillingness to exert itself. It is not a sign that a person is spiritually ruined, and it goes against plain common sense, which is why it is easier to beat than it looks. Most of the time it comes with excuses: "my memory is weak", "the desire was taken from me", "I'm not capable". The Rebbe calls these the yetzer's pretexts, some of them odd. It also comes dressed as piety, as brooding over one's faults. His test is what the thoughts produce: if they breed sadness, slackness or despair, they are not from the good side. Sadness sits closer to laziness than to alacrity, and despair slackens a person more than complacency does. He does not merge this with tiredness. Care for the body is a mitzvah. Not every health complaint is the yetzer. Real fatigue calls for rest or a change of activity. The Rayatz adds how the slide works: it starts with skipping fixed times for trivial reasons, moves into self-deception, and ends in a deep sleep. He also warns that seeing one's own lowliness can turn into melancholy and laziness. Toras Menachem names staleness, where the work has grown old and feels like a heavy yoke.

## Real cases from the letters

- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (24 Tammuz 5715; 4 Adar I 5719).** *Two men wrote, separately, that they suffer from laziness and asked for a remedy.* — Learn the opening of Tanya ch. 41 (up to 'before the King') carefully and know it by heart. Go over it from time to time, aloud or at least in thought. Once it is engraved in the mind and reaches the heart, it will drive out the laziness and add alacrity. Alacrity comes from joy and an open heart. Since it was commanded, the ability was given.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (24 Kislev 5714).** *A man was taking stock of himself, felt low about his personal situation, and was not doing his own avodah.* — Your situation doesn't justify doing nothing, but sadness is closer to laziness than to alacrity. So start with something practical, learning, even purely as an obligation: a short time every day and longer when you are freer. Once there is a seed of growth, it is easier to bring it to full action.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (11 Iyar 5717).** *A young man left yeshiva, took a teaching job and then dropped it, now wants to try farming, and calls his spiritual state 'terrible'.* — The common thread is not wanting to exert yourself. It is not a terrible state, just laziness, which goes against simple common sense and so is easier to beat. Before you choose, find out from people in the field what the work involves, then work with vigor. Don't expect miracles in a few days; expect 'you toiled and you found'.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1 Elul 5717).** *A man completed the courses but did not go to the exams, citing a weak memory.* — Not going makes no sense, since there was nothing to lose. The memory claim is the yetzer's advice, meant to drop a person into laziness, and it hunts for pretexts. Go at least to the next exams, do what is needed to succeed, and put the idea that you are 'not capable' out of your mind entirely.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (16 MarCheshvan 5718).** *A student wrote that he wants to learn but has no desire for it, as if the desire had been taken from him.* — The desire cannot be taken from you. This impression is one of the yetzer's tricks, and it does not shy away from falsehood. Fight it by paying it no attention: don't enter into debate with it, answer with one short phrase: there is no time for debates, I am a servant busy with my Master's command.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (16 MarCheshvan 5718).** *A man who had been advised to work in agriculture wrote that he has no desire at all for any part of it.* — This point matters. Sustained work cannot be run in an orderly way if it rests only on duty, because will and pleasure are what drive all the faculties. He should consult his friends again about the choice.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (2 Iyar 5715; 5 Tammuz 5718).** *A teacher felt exhausted after teaching. A woman who had gone back to work after a long break was worn out by her job and her home.* — To the teacher: arrange a short break in the middle, or come in properly rested; when you take hold of the natural means, success comes beyond nature too. To the woman: this is probably just the starting-up period. Step by step she will settle into a track and organize her time, the fatigue will fade, and the satisfaction of the work will speed that up.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (15 Teves 5711).** *A young man full of doubts, unable to concentrate and feeling empty, was turning to amulets and fortune-tellers.* — His inability to concentrate is only the animal soul's laziness, its unwillingness to exert itself. For some weeks, stop assessing your spiritual state and be joyful. Learn aloud, from a book or in writing. After about an hour, stop for five or ten minutes, then continue. Remember that a healthy body is one of God's ways. Don't eat an hour before sleep.

## False conclusions and their corrections

- **“I'm not capable. My memory is weak. The desire has been taken out of me.”** — The letters call these the yetzer's pretexts and advise against arguing with them. Since the task was commanded, the ability to do it was given.
- **“My state is terrible. I'm spiritually ruined.”** — This is laziness, an unwillingness to exert oneself, not some grand evil. Because it goes against plain common sense, it is easier to overcome.
- **“Dwelling on my failures is the serious, pious thing to do.”** — If the thinking breeds sadness, slackness or despair, it comes from the yetzer dressed as piety. Real remorse adds vigor.
- **“Every time I'm tired it's laziness, so I should push through or break myself.”** — Health is a mitzvah and not every health complaint is the yetzer. When tired, switch to a different kind of task or rest; the rest itself prepares the work. The body is to be refined, not broken.
- **“I'll start when I have more time.”** — Once a person starts, it turns out within a short time that the capacity is there, and it grows. Don't push to tomorrow what can be done today.
- **“If I switch to a different field, things will just work out.”** — Don't fool yourself that you will see open miracles within a few days. Find out what the work involves, then put in real effort.

## Programs

**Start with a small act, daily** 1. Pick one concrete activity (in the letter, learning). 2. Do it for a short time every day, even purely out of obligation and without feeling. 3. Do it for longer when you have more free time. 4. Let the small daily practice grow; once the seed exists, fuller action comes more easily.

**Memorize a short passage of standing before the King** 1. Learn the opening of Tanya ch. 41 carefully, up to the words 'before the King'. 2. Know it by heart. 3. Go over the idea, or the words themselves, from time to time, aloud or at least in thought. 4. Contemplate even its simple meaning until it is engraved in the mind and reaches the heart.

**One long contemplation, then short recall** 1. Contemplate once, for a long stretch, that a person does not sit and act alone at home the way he does before a great king. 2. Afterwards, whenever alacrity needs strengthening, recall only the headings of that contemplation. 3. Let the recall remind you that God stands over you, and get up out of the slackness with alacrity.

**Don't debate the voice** 1. When the thought comes that the desire is gone or that you can't, don't argue with it or analyse it. 2. Answer with one short phrase: there is no time for debates, I am busy with what I was asked to do. 3. Go back to the task.

**A working rhythm with rests** 1. For a few weeks, stop auditing your inner state. 2. Learn aloud, from a book or in writing. 3. After about an hour, stop for five to ten minutes, to rest or to go over what you learned, then continue. 4. When fatigue comes, switch to a different kind of task or rest for a while. 5. Keep the body healthy (e.g., don't eat in the hour before sleep); come to the work rested.

## Reframes

- I'm spiritually ruined → This is just unwillingness to make an effort, and it goes against plain sense, so it can be beaten *(It shrinks the problem from a verdict about who I am to one habit I can deal with)* - I'm too down to start → Sadness sits nearer to not-moving than to moving, so move a little *(The low mood is treated as the thing keeping me stuck, not as a reason to wait)* - The desire has been taken from me → That impression is a trick, and it doesn't deserve a debate *(Refusing to argue takes away the excuse's power)* - Resting means I'm failing → Rest and a change of task are preparation for the work, and strength is renewed each day *(It separates real fatigue from laziness, so guilt doesn't pile onto tiredness)* - I have no time → Start, and the capacity shows up and grows *(Action comes first and the sense of having room follows)* - It's a heavy yoke → It has gone stale and needs warmth and life put back into it *(The heaviness is read as coldness; work done with vitality goes faster than work done in sadness and sluggishness)* - I must be either fully satisfied or in despair → Both extremes weaken effort, and despair weakens it more *(It watches the mood for what it does to the work)*

## Counsel in worldly matters

In these letters the Rebbe sends people to practical, ordinary means. Take breaks and arrive rested ("taking hold of nature"). Keep the body healthy, as a religious duty. Don't write off health complaints as the yetzer. Prefer work that gives inner satisfaction with less nervous strain and fatigue. Ask people who work in a field what the work involves before choosing it. Consult friends again about a path you have no desire for. Show up to the exam. I found no letter on this condition that sends the person to a doctor. The guide should still check for health causes of persistent low energy and refer.

## Where the Rebbeim differ

Within the Rebbe's letters: he says to start even out of pure obligation, and also that long-term work cannot rest on obligation alone and needs desire. Read together, obligation begins the work and desire has to carry it. The Rayatz maamarim trace the slide into sleep and self-deception and treat melancholy about one's faults as a cause. Hayom Yom (Besht) warns against breaking the body. One Toras Menachem hit (not verified) takes a harsher line on laziness in understanding: "beat the wood".

## Cautions

- Persistent heaviness, exhaustion or loss of desire can be depression, illness or sleep deprivation. Don't call it 'just laziness' or 'the yetzer' before asking about sleep, health and mood. Refer to a doctor or mental-health professional when it persists. - The 'excuse of the yetzer' frame came from specific letters to specific people. Don't use it to dismiss a real, assessed limitation (memory, learning, health). - Don't prescribe long self-audits: the letters warn that stocktaking which breeds sadness or despair is harmful. - The 'standing before the King' contemplations can feed fear in anxious or scrupulous people. - No self-mortification: the body is to be refined, not broken. - In one letter the Rebbe advises that a person should not choose a long-term path with no desire at all for it. Don't push someone into sustained work on willpower alone.

## Candidate truths

**The seed comes before the feeling** (`seed_before_feeling`, fidelity: yes)

You don't wait to feel like it. A small real act, done every day even without desire, plants a seed, and once there is a seed, growth gets easier.

> Critic: Q04: sadness is near laziness, so start learning even as a duty, briefly daily and longer when free, and a seed eases growth. Q10: long-term work can't run on duty alone. Q13 (context: a teacher's fatigue after a break) is about tiredness easing step by step, not laziness. Q04's act is Torah study; say so.

**The excuse is not a verdict** (`excuse_is_not_verdict`, fidelity: yes)

The voice that says 'you're not capable, the desire is gone, not now' is not the truth about you. Don't debate it. Answer it briefly and go back to the task. Real tiredness is different: that calls for rest.

> Critic: Q08: 'not capable' is the evil inclination's pretext, so put it aside and act. Q09: don't debate, one short line. Q18: thoughts that breed laziness or despair come from the evil inclination. Q11: real fatigue calls for rest or a change of task. Faithful.

**You are not alone in the room** (`standing_before`, fidelity: yes)

A person sits and moves differently when they know they are standing before someone great. Holding that awareness for a long stretch once, then recalling it briefly, lifts a person out of slackness.

> Critic: Q01: learn Tanya 41 opening ('before the King') by heart and review it, which drives out laziness. Q02: review until engraved. Q23: contemplate once at length, then recall its headings, and rise with alacrity. Faithful.

## Gaps

I found no letter that sends a chronically sluggish person to a doctor, or that looks at sleep, diet or medical causes beyond general health care. There is nothing on procrastination driven by fear of failure or perfectionism, or on attention disorders. The Tanya ch. 41 remedy assumes a religious Jew who learns Tanya. The Rayatz letters yielded nothing usable, and Admur HaEmtzai was thin. Most cases are men in learning or communal work; few are women or people in secular jobs.

## B16 · I hurt someone and want to make it right

*I said or did something that hurt someone, maybe years ago. I feel sick about it. I've prayed about it, but it still sits there. I don't know if I can face them, or I can't even find them now, and I don't know what would actually fix it.*

**Coverage.** Planned probes (לבקש מחילה, בין אדם לחבירו, לפייס, מחילה מחבירו), plus my own (בקשת מחילה, בקש מחילה, פגיעה בכבוד, בפני עשרה, עד שירצה, פצויים, גרם צער), counted in the Rebbe's letters, Toras Menachem, the Alter Rebbe's letters and the Rayatz letters. I grepped the hits in the Rebbe's letters (about 60 lines), in Toras Menachem (27) and in the Alter Rebbe's letters (5). I read about 20 letters and 6 talk passages closely. The Rayatz letters had 4 thin hits. Derech Mitzvosecha only defines confession. Hayom Yom had 0 hits.

**What the sources do.** treats it: the Rebbe's letters give concrete, repeated procedure for asking forgiveness, including when the person cannot be found or has died, and the talks state the principle and its limits; the inner, emotional side of guilt after hurting someone is thin.

## The reading

The Rebbe (talks) holds that a wrong against a person is not mended by repentance before God alone: you have to appease the person and give back what was taken. If you truly did all you could and they refuse or cannot be found, God accepts your repentance, but damage left in the world still stands. In the letters he treats asking forgiveness as a practical task: done wholeheartedly, made known to everyone who knew of the wrong, and with the harm repaired and money paid where that applies. When the person cannot be reached it is said before ten people (or three). He admits it is hard on a person's nature. In disputes he insists neither side is 100% right. The Alter Rebbe asks a man to accept being the insulted one and apologise in public for the sake of peace.

## Real cases from the letters

- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 6970, 2 Shevat 5719).** *A young woman writes about her quick temper and anger, alongside trouble between her parents.* — Have your teachers explain divine providence until it is engraved in memory. Also keep the rule that whoever you hurt, even in anger, you must ask for full forgiveness. Because apologising is hard, remembering you will have to do it will help weaken the anger.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 5777, 3 Tishrei 5718).** *A man was reported to have struck a teacher who does religious education; he has likely apologised privately.* — Asking forgiveness is required by the Torah, but it must be made known to everyone who knew of the incident. The letter was sent urgently so it could be settled before Yom Kippur.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 5152, 21 Shevat 5717).** *A single woman says that years ago, in a quarrel, she said improper words to another woman who now lives abroad and cannot be found.* — Before ten people, name the woman and ask her forgiveness if she wronged or shamed her; there is no need to give the details, and the ten reply that it is forgiven. Also repair any consequences of that quarrel if she can.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 1661, 25 Sivan 5712).** *A man in a second marriage with poor domestic peace. His first wife may have been pressed into the divorce, and he does not know where she is now.* — If the divorce was forced, gather a minyan and ask her forgiveness before them in her absence. Also work on letting things pass in his own character toward his present wife.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 1150, 25 Menachem Av 5711).** *A former cemetery caretaker, now in poor health, may have slighted the honour of the dead there.* — Go with three (or ten) people and ask forgiveness there, saying his intent was only for their honour. But only once his doctors allow him to walk the streets and go there.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 4291, 4 Iyar 5717).** *Written to a community torn by quarrels, where each side demands that the other repent and be humble.* — When there are two sides, it is almost impossible for one to be 100% right. Each is at least partly at fault. Stop urging repentance on others with passion while excusing yourselves with equal passion.
- **The Alter Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 56).** *A man in a dispute with a wealthy communal figure, possibly with right on his side, before Yom Kippur.* — Pass over your traits and be one of the insulted, and ask him for forgiveness in public before Yom Kippur, for my sake and for the sake of peace. Most troubles come from disputes waged for the sake of Heaven.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (17 Adar 5720).** *A man quarrelled with someone who spoke strangely; the other has since written asking forgiveness and wants to reconnect.* — Since he has apologised, the relationship should not just return to what it was but grow beyond it. How to get there (a pause or another way) should be worked out with friends who know the man.

## False conclusions and their corrections

- **“I've repented to God and prayed about it, so it's settled.”** — In matters between people it is not enough to repent privately or even to be forgiven by Heaven; you must appease the person and return what you took.
- **“They won't forgive me (or I can't find them), so I'm guilty forever.”** — If you truly did all in your power and they refuse or cannot be found, your repentance is accepted. For someone unreachable, the apology is made before others. You ask up to three times, not endlessly.
- **“I was mostly right, so it's their job to come to me.”** — In a two-sided quarrel it is almost never 100% one way. Excusing yourself while demanding humility from the other is the reverse of the path. The Alter Rebbe asked a man to apologise in public even in a dispute for the sake of Heaven.
- **“A quiet private sorry covers a public wrong.”** — The apology has to be known to everyone who knew of the wrong. Publishing an apology at once is called the proper conduct.
- **“I should punish myself for it.”** — The Rebbe rejected harmful fasting as not the Chassidic way. The harder and truer work is in speech and in loving even someone who has wronged you.

## Programs

**Asking forgiveness when the person can be reached** 1. Go to the person and ask for complete forgiveness, even if the hurt was done in anger. 2. If the wrong was known to others, make sure the apology becomes known to everyone who knew of it. 3. Repair the consequences of the hurt where you can, return what was taken, and pay money compensation where that applies. 4. If refused, ask again; after three sincere requests you need not keep asking. 5. Afterwards, work toward closeness with gentle words and warm acts, not with rational arguments alone, until the relationship is better than before.

**When the person cannot be found or has died** 1. Ask a rabbi whether an apology is called for in this case and how. 2. Gather ten people (or at least three). 3. Name the person; details of what happened need not be told. 4. Say that if you wronged them or harmed their honour, knowingly or not, you regret it wholeheartedly and ask forgiveness. 5. The listeners reply that it is forgiven. 6. Still repair any consequences of the old wrong that are in your power.

**Letting the cost of apology curb anger** 1. Take on the rule that whoever you hurt, even in anger, you will ask for full forgiveness. 2. Actually do it each time, despite how hard it is. 3. When anger rises, remember that you will have to humble yourself and apologise afterwards; let that hold you back. 4. Alongside this, have a teacher explain divine providence until it is fixed in memory.

## Reframes

- Apologising is humiliating and I can't bring myself to do it. → It is hard by nature, and that very hardness, once faced, becomes a brake on the anger that caused the hurt. *(The expected cost of humbling yourself is remembered in the next moment of anger.)* - My inner repentance should be enough. → The wrong lives in the world between two people, so it is mended there: with the person and by repairing the damage. *(It moves the work from the conscience to concrete action toward the one who was hurt.)* - If they won't forgive, I can never be free. → Your part is to do all you can; their refusal is not your guilt, though real damage should still be repaired. *(It separates what is in your power from what is not.)* - I was in the right. → Almost no quarrel has one side 100% right; for peace, be willing to be the one who yields. *(Looking honestly at your own share of the fault.)* - I must punish myself for what I did. → Self-harm is not the way; restraining your tongue and loving even people who wronged you is harder and does not damage your health. *(It turns guilt from self-punishment into acts that face other people.)*

## Counsel in worldly matters

The letters send the person to real people. A rabbi judges whether an apology is needed and how (Q28). Friends who know the other person advise how to reconcile (Q29). Community elders help make peace between spouses. Parents' friends are asked to soften a father. Teachers explain providence to a girl with a temper. Doctors must permit an ill man to go out before he goes to apologise (Q10). Money compensation is weighed (Q07). Guilt is never treated as a medical matter in what I read.

## Where the Rebbeim differ

In many letters about childlessness, a hard birth, bad dreams or marriage strain, the Rebbe asks whether an old broken engagement shamed someone and prescribes an apology. That is his individual counsel, not a general cause the guide should assume. In a talk he held back from ordering an apology because a forced one would be half-hearted and would pain the other person. The Alter Rebbe asks for a public apology even from someone who may be in the right.

## Cautions

- Never tell a person that their illness, infertility or misfortune is caused by someone they once hurt; that causal reading was the Rebbe's individual counsel, not something a guide can apply. - Do not push anyone to contact or apologise to a person who has asked for no contact, or where contact would reopen harm; a forced or half-hearted apology can pain the other person again. - Not for victims of abuse being pressured to 'own their part', forgive, or apologise to the one who harmed them; the 'both sides share the fault' teaching was written to a community in a quarrel, not to someone who was harmed. - For someone caught in scrupulous, endless guilt or repeated apologising, stress the limits: do what is in your power, ask up to three times, then it is complete; consider referral if guilt is obsessive. - Where the harm involves violence, money or legal matters, restitution is required and the person should get proper rabbinic and, where needed, legal advice. - Where health is involved, doctors' permission comes first, as the Rebbe required.

## Candidate truths

**The wrong is mended with the person you wronged** (`mend_it_with_them`, fidelity: yes)

A hurt done to another person is repaired between you and them: going to them, owning it wholeheartedly, and making good what can be made good. Private remorse and self-punishment do not replace that.

> Critic: Q22: repentance or Heaven's pardon is not enough, one must appease the person. Q21/Q04/Q07: ask forgiveness, return, pay. Q02: make it known to those who knew. Q01/Q13. Q16 (context 51778) is about fasting in general, not making amends. Fix: drop Q16 or cite it only for 'no self-punishment'.

**Your part is what is in your power** (`your_part_is_what_you_can_do`, fidelity: yes)

When you have honestly done all you can to make amends and the other person will not forgive or cannot be found, their refusal is not your guilt. What you still owe is to repair any real damage you can.

> Critic: Q18: all one could do, and refusal or absence is not one's fault. Q19: damage done must still be repaired. Q27: ask three times. Q03/Q05/Q28: before ten Jews if the person can't be found or has died. Faithful.

**A quarrel almost never has one side wholly right** (`rarely_one_side`, fidelity: yes)

In a falling-out between two sides, each almost always carries some of the fault. Owning your share, and being willing to yield for the sake of peace, opens the way back.

> Critic: Q11: in this world almost never is one side 100% right. Q12: people demand others repent while excusing themselves. Q23/Q24 (Alter Rebbe): let things pass, accept insult, ask pardon publicly for peace. Q13: win a spouse with warmth, not arguments. Faithful.

## Gaps

I found nothing on guilt or self-reproach that lingers after amends are made, on forgiving oneself, on the wording of a direct apology, on apologising to one's own children, or on wrongs to non-Jews (the unreachable-person rite is framed as before ten Jews). Nothing on harm too grave to be forgiven, or where contact is unsafe. The Rayatz letters, Hayom Yom and Derech Mitzvosecha add almost nothing here.

## B17 · I can't let go of a grudge / I keep judging people

*He hurt me and I replay it. I won't go to their simcha, I've stopped doing my part, I keep a list of what they did. When I look at people I see mostly what's wrong with them, and I can't find anything in them to love.*

**Coverage.** Rebbe's Igros (IK): counted ~35 probes. Planned ones (אהבת ישראל 314, לא תשנא 2, הריני מוחל 1) were mostly off-target. Productive ones: טינא, ברוגז, נעלב/עלבון, למחול, תרעומות, טענות על, שנאה, בעין טובה, ללמד זכות. Grepped about 110 hits and read about 15 letters in context. Toras Menachem: grepped עד שתגיע למקומו, רואה רע בזולתו, טינא בלב, הריני מוחל, נטירה; read 4 talks. Rayatz Igros: טינא, לא תשנא, שנאת חנם; read the 1931 letter. Hayom Yom and Sefer HaSichos Rayatz: counts and skims only. Derech Mitzvosecha checked, not re-read. Letters of the Alter Rebbe, Mitteler Rebbe, Tzemach Tzedek and Rashab not searched. About 26 lines verified.

**What the sources do.** treats it, sparsely: about eight letters answer a real person's grudge. Habitual judging of others is covered mostly in public talks, not in letters to individuals.

## The reading

The Rebbe's letters treat a grudge as an unfinished account the hurt person keeps open, and he tells them to close it for their own sake. He grounds this in divine providence (he points to Iggeres HaKodesh 25 again and again): what is truly decreed for you cannot be taken by another person, so there is no reason to hate him. He also insists the person's own life and duty are his own; leaving them because someone else did wrong only harms yourself. For forgiveness he appeals to the fact that we ask G-d to forgive us with no trace left. In a talk he describes two kinds of love: love that comes from feeling breaks when the other goes against you, while love that comes from understanding puts itself in the other's place and fills with compassion. The talks add the Baal Shem Tov's teaching that the wrong you see in another is shown to you to fix, perhaps in yourself. The Rebbe Rayatz adds the Torah's order: do not hate him in your heart; speak to him calmly and ask why he did it.

## Real cases from the letters

- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (8 Elul 5715).** *Someone who had been insulted was asked for forgiveness by the people who wronged him, and by all reports he refused.* — If the report is true: he is reminded of the Torah law on a person asked to forgive an insult, and of the Chassidic teachings on it. In Elul we ask G-d to forgive a whole year with no trace left; how much more should a man forgive another man. The Rebbe adds that the report may be wrong and asks to hear good news.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (Cheshvan 5716).** *A man running a synagogue let it slide because of grievances against the members who were not helping him as he wanted.* — Drop all the accounts and grievances and look at the matter as it truly is: your own spiritual and material concern. Be grateful to those who help. If someone does not help, that is no reason to neglect what concerns you and your family. There is no place for keeping score or feuding when the matter is your own.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (10 Kislev 5717).** *A person on bad terms with a relative asked whether to go to that relative's wedding.* — Feuding has no place, especially for someone who believes in divine providence; be sure that G-d meant it for good (see Iggeres HaKodesh 25). So take part in a fellow Jew's joy, especially a relative's. How long to stay, only the chuppah or later too, should be worked out with local community members.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (6 Tishrei 5718).** *A village community after a violent incident in which someone raised a hand against another; the wronged side felt aggrieved.* — Postscript: following the Sages on one who causes another to stumble, and Iggeres HaKodesh 25, even those who were offended should take on extra care in G-d's commandments, and certainly uproot every trace of grudge from their heart.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (answer to questions, Tishrei 5710).** *A questioner asked what it means to truly 'work on the emotions', with the example of a man who hates a competitor in his own trade and district.* — Two stages. First the mind argues: if G-d decreed your livelihood, the rival cannot take it; if less was decreed, it would have come another way; so why hate him? Argue until the hatred stops in deed, speech and thought. Then love your fellow: help him with advice or a loan, until the hatred turns into love.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (22 Teves 5721).** *A correspondent wrote to the Rebbe about hatred (the letter does not say whose). The Rebbe read it against a general disorder in how he was living.* — The Rebbe was alarmed: this is empty talk. It comes only from a lack of order in his life, which weakens bodily health and reduces good spirits and joy. Order is needed in eating, drinking, sleep and livelihood. The change need not happen all at once, but he must decide it is his goal and start at once, step by step. For the practical steps, consult friends.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (15 Iyar 5714).** *A young rabbi at odds with lay members of his community, feeling that he was suffering at their hands.* — They do this at most from lack of knowledge, not lack of fear of Heaven, so he must draw them close. Because he was privileged to study and receive guidance, the initiative and the first steps, and perhaps more, must come from him. That is no loss of dignity and no slight to the honor of Torah.
- **The Rebbe Rayatz, Igros Kodesh (1931).** *People in a town who opposed the Chabad Chassidim over the rabbinate and leadership, with hard, contentious men among them.* — The Torah says: do not hate your brother in your heart and speak to him in anger. Instead rebuke him pleasantly: ask him why he did it and explain the harm, so he can explain himself or regret it. The Rebbe Rayatz writes that for their forefathers' sake he disregards everything done until now, and asks them to give up their contentiousness.

## False conclusions and their corrections

- **“He wronged me, so I'm entitled to stop doing my part and pull away.”** — Your life and responsibilities belong to you, not to him. It makes no sense that because someone wronged you, you should harm yourself.
- **“He took what was mine, so of course I hate him.”** — What is decreed for you no rival can take. If less was decreed, it would have come another way. So he took nothing from you, and there is no reason to hate him.
- **“I need to keep making my complaint against them.”** — Complaints against others carry two doubts: whether you are right, and whether it will help, since they are free not to listen. Complaints against yourself have neither doubt.
- **“I can't love someone I find no merit in.”** — Love without cause is the cure for hatred without cause. You love him even when you have tried and found no merit. Put yourself in his place and compassion comes.
- **“If I forgive, I'm letting it go too cheaply.”** — We ask G-d to forgive a whole year so that no trace is left. How much more should a person forgive a person who asks.
- **“What's wrong with him has nothing to do with me.”** — In the talks: what you are shown in another is shown for fixing, and it may point to something in you. (For judging, not for someone who was harmed.)

## Programs

**Two stages with a resented rival** 1. Notice the hatred rising against the rival. 2. Argue with your mind: if my living was decreed, he cannot take it; if less was decreed, it would have come another way; so why hate him? 3. Keep adding arguments until the hatred stops showing in deed, then in speech, and finally even in thought. 4. Do not stop there: help him, with good advice, a loan or the like. 5. In the end the hatred turns into love.

**Say it to him, calmly, instead of storing it** 1. When someone has done you wrong, do not keep the hatred hidden in your heart. 2. Do not speak to him in anger. 3. Ask him pleasantly why he did it, and explain what harm it caused. 4. Let him explain himself or regret it; that is how the matter can end in peace. 5. Where you can, speak face to face rather than in writing, so the other can answer on the spot and no grudge is left.

**Move the time spent on complaints to yourself** 1. Notice how much time goes into complaints against others. 2. Remember the two doubts: are you right, and will it help, since he is free not to listen? 3. Spend that same time on complaints against yourself, improving your own situation, where you have both the right and the power.

**Order in life for someone consumed by hatred** 1. Treat the hateful talk as coming from disorder in your life, which weakens health and joy. 2. Bring order to eating, drinking and sleep, and on to livelihood. 3. You need not change completely at once, but decide that this is your goal. 4. Start at once, step by step. 5. Work out the practical steps with friends.

**Put yourself in his place** 1. When someone goes against your wishes, the feeling in your heart finds nothing to love. 2. Use your mind instead: do not judge him until you have stood in his place. 3. Put yourself into his situation and understand what brought him to act this way, and judge him favorably. 4. When you do this properly, you fill with compassion for him, and that adds to the love. 5. If you still find no merit, love him anyway: love without cause.

**Take the first step** 1. Recognize that the other side may act from lack of knowledge, not bad will. 2. Make the first move to draw them close; that is no loss of dignity. 3. Where there is a family joy, take part in it and do not stay away. 4. Settle practical details, such as how long to stay, with people who know the local situation.

## Reframes

- He took what was mine. → No one can take what is decreed for me. *(Divine providence removes the rival as the cause of the loss, so the hatred has nothing to stand on.)* - My grudge is my business with him. → My life and my duties are my own, not his. *(Separates your own affairs from the other person's wrong, so the grudge stops costing you your own good.)* - I can only love him if he deserves it. → Love without cause cures hatred without cause. *(Following Rambam, cure one extreme by leaning to the other; understanding his place brings compassion.)* - I see what's wrong with him. → I was shown this in order to fix it, perhaps in me. *(The Baal Shem Tov's teaching turns judging into self-examination and fixing, not condemning.)* - The one who was hurt has every right to hold on. → The wronged can hold no grudge at all and even seek the other's good, as Joseph did with his brothers. *(A model from the Torah of someone badly wronged who kept no grudge and repaid good.)* - I see them as they are. → How I look at them shapes how I treat and speak to them. *(Looking with a good eye changes your conduct and speech, and closeness achieves more than the opposite.)*

## Counsel in worldly matters

For someone consumed by hatred, the Rebbe prescribes order in eating, drinking, sleep and livelihood, begun step by step, with practical steps worked out with friends: "salvation lies in much counsel." For a dispute: talk face to face rather than in writing, so the other can answer. He sends a wedding-attendance question to local community members. The Rebbe Rayatz says to speak to the offender directly and calmly. No doctor or psychologist referral was found for this condition.

## Where the Rebbeim differ

The Rebbe Rayatz (following Ramban) stresses confronting the offender gently, asking why, so the matter can be resolved. The Rebbe's letters stress closing the account inwardly: providence, your own duty, forgiving when asked. One letter also says complaints against others are doubtful to help. The Baal Shem Tov's mirror teaching appears in the Rebbe's public talks, not in letters to individuals who were wronged.

## Cautions

- Do not apply the mirror teaching ('what you see in another is in you') to someone who was abused or harmed. It is taught about judging, and the letters to wronged people never tell them it was their own fault. - The providence reframe does not mean the wrongdoer is blameless, or that the person should stay exposed to harm. One letter is explicit that you should not harm yourself because another wronged you. - The letters on forgiving concern someone who asked for forgiveness, or a feud between equals. They do not address abuse, ongoing danger or unrepentant harm; there, safety and a trusted person come first. - If the resentment includes thoughts of revenge, violence or loss of control, the guide should send the person to a person or professional at once. The letters do not treat this. - 'Take the first step' and 'help your rival' are not demands to trust or get close to someone dangerous.

## Candidate truths

**No one can take what is yours** (`cannot_take_yours`, fidelity: yes)

What is truly meant for you cannot be taken by another person, so the grudge guards nothing. Letting go loses you nothing that was yours.

> Critic: Q09 (context 10878: a business competitor): what is meant for you he cannot take, so why hate him; the passage goes on to helping him. Q04: feuding is pointless, providence meant it for good. Q03 is about the shul being his own duty, not about loss. Fix: drop Q03 here.

**Their wrong does not get to run your life** (`your_life_is_yours`, fidelity: yes)

What they did is on their account. Your work, your duties and your good remain yours, and giving them up because of someone else is a second hurt you do to yourself.

> Critic: Q03 (context 48420: shul work): drop the accounts with others, it is your own matter. Q06: don't harm yourself because someone wronged you. Q11: complaints against others waste time, doubtful if right and doubtful if it helps. Q04. Faithful.

**Stand where they stand** (`stand_in_their_place`, fidelity: yes)

Love that depends on feeling breaks when the other crosses you. Love that comes from understanding puts itself in their place, fills with compassion, and can love even where it finds no merit.

> Critic: Q19: 'don't judge until you reach his place', and putting yourself in their place brings compassion that adds to love. Q18: love even without finding merit. Q13: a good eye shapes conduct and speech. Q22: seeing evil shows evil in oneself. Faithful.

## Gaps

No letter found on abuse, betrayal by a spouse or parent, or long family estrangement. None on forgiving someone who never apologizes (only the Rayatz's gentle rebuke and the model of Joseph). No doctor or therapist referral found. Habitual judgmentalism appears mostly in public talks, not in letters to individuals. Letters of the earlier Rebbeim (Alter Rebbe to Rashab) were not searched. One letter's 'hatred' is unclear as to whose it is.

## B18 · Envy and constantly comparing myself to others

*Someone else's good news makes me sick inside. My friend learns better, my neighbour has the nicer house, their kids are doing better. I keep score all day and never come out ahead. I can't enjoy what I have because I'm always looking at what they have.*

**Coverage.** Planned probes (עין טובה, קנאת סופרים, עין רעה, חלקו בחייו), plus קנאה, מקנא, מתקנא, שמח בחלקו, צר עין, שחבריו, בהשוואה, אין פרצופיהם דומים. I counted them in the Rebbe's letters (קנאה 39, קנאת סופרים 33, שמח בחלקו 12), Toras Menachem (קנאה 159, מתקנא 12), the Rayatz letters (8), the Alter Rebbe letters (13), Hayom Yom and Rayatz Sichos. I grepped about 150 hits and read closely about 12 letters, 5 talk passages, 1 Rayatz letter and 1 Rayatz story. About 26 tool calls, roughly 60k characters read.

**What the sources do.** thin: the Rebbe answers about six people who wrote about envy or comparison and gives one strong talk on keeping up with the neighbours, but there is no worked-out treatment, and the Alter Rebbe's letters mention envy only as communal persecution.

## The reading

The Rebbe treats envy as raw force, neither good nor bad in itself. Aimed at another person's learning or goodness, it is the scholars' envy that increases wisdom. That means it should be increased and turned into more effort of one's own. Aimed anywhere else, it stays base. With the Tzemach Tzedek, he calls it a holy worm that rots the animal soul's traits. For material things the Rebbe reverses the direction. Look up to those above you in spiritual matters and down to those below you in material ones, and you become content with your lot. In a talk he describes keeping up with the neighbour as an endless chase. It eats up nights, mind and heart, and leaves no time for children or for the soul's purpose. He calls it 'a service foreign to you'. In a letter he says people differ in mind as they do in face, and each finds fulfilment in his own field where he stands out, not on the common scale. The Rayatz: everyone carries a native flaw, one of which is envy, and the other person has private pains you do not see.

## Real cases from the letters

- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 6658, 24 Teves 5719).** *A yeshiva student writes that he suffers from envy of friends who study more diligently than he does, though he too longs to study with diligence.* — The Rebbe is surprised at his tone. The Sages say scholars' envy increases wisdom, so he should increase this trait and use it to increase his own diligence, and his success will grow. He also tells him to add chassidus study and to keep the three daily lessons (Chumash, Tehillim, Tanya).
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (13 Tishrei 5715).** *A man who has wandered for years writes that he envied Lubavitcher chassidim he met, who live their own way wherever they go, as if in their own four cubits.* — Envy is generally a base trait, but holy envy, scholars' envy, increases wisdom. Otherwise it stays base. So his feeling should become strengthening his own Torah and mitzvos with beauty, and throwing himself into Jewish work where he now lives. Perhaps everything so far was preparation for that.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 1437, Rosh Chodesh Adar 5712, Yiddish).** *A father who trusts G-d about his livelihood consoles himself about his children's religious conduct by saying they are better than others.* — The Rebbe turns the comparison around. In heavenly matters look upward to higher people and strive toward them. In material matters look downward at those who have less. That makes one content with one's lot, the true rich man. He adds: keep speaking gently with the children.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 1001, 10 Iyar 5711).** *A young woman is offered several matches and cannot decide, for lack of real satisfaction. Part of her worry is how the match looks to outsiders.* — A house is built for the people who live in it, not for passers-by in the street. What passers-by think of outward 'beauty' is how people by nature measure contentment or its opposite, but it is secondary to a firm foundation of Torah and fear of Heaven.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 8439, 16 Sivan 5722).** *A young man asks why his friends rise easily in Torah and fear of Heaven while he does not.* — We are promised that one who sanctifies himself a little below is sanctified much from above, so if he makes the effort he will succeed. Searching for such questions is not the way, except at rare intervals. The main thing is to learn steadily and diligently and keep the daily lessons.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 8885, days of Selichos 5724).** *A public figure asks about collective life, where a group seems to equalise small and great.* — People differ in mind as in face. The individual finds satisfaction by fully bringing out what is in him in his own field, where he stands out. Cooperation removes the unwanted envy and rivalry that spoil relations. Competition is not to be abolished, since it drives effort, but channelled to a higher plane.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 6270, 24 Iyar 5718).** *A yeshiva head reports on student examinations.* — The Rebbe hopes the examination stirred scholars' envy in the students, which increases wisdom. He cites the Tzemach Tzedek: 'one who has envy rots' means a holy worm that rots the cedar, that is, the animal soul's traits.
- **The Rebbe Rayatz, Igros Kodesh (15 Teves [5]682).** *A chassid is told how to improve relations with others.* — Look for the good in others and learn this from yourself. Everyone knows his own afflictions: one is a liar by nature, one hot-tempered, one envious, one cold. Breaking a trait takes long, hard work. Seen this way, the other person also has his private pains, which do not show on the outside.

## False conclusions and their corrections

- **“If I only get what he has, I'll be at peace.”** — The chase has no end. Whoever has a hundred wants two hundred, and one antique chair only makes you equal, so you need two. It eats mind, heart and nights.
- **“I have to keep up with the people around me or I'm nothing.”** — Give up the desire to compete with the neighbours and a few hours' work will cover your needs, leaving time for what matters. Chasing the neighbour is 'a service foreign to you', not your business.
- **“Feeling envy shows I'm a bad person.”** — Everyone carries a native flaw, and envy is one of them. Envy is raw material: turned toward the other's good qualities it increases wisdom. Only left as it is does it stay base.
- **“How it looks to others is the measure of whether my life is good.”** — By nature people measure contentment by what passers-by think, but that is secondary to the foundation the life is built on.
- **“Things come easily to them and never to me; it's unfair.”** — Don't dwell on 'why them'. A little effort from below draws much help from above. The other also has private pains you cannot see.

## Programs

**Turn the envy into your own effort** 1. Notice what exactly you envy in the other person: their diligence, learning, way of life, goodness. 2. Do not treat the feeling as shameful. Where its object is a good quality, increase it on purpose, using it to push your own effort in that same thing. 3. Take concrete action: study more steadily, keep your observance with care, and add study of chassidus. 4. Do not keep asking why it comes easily to them and not to you; take up such questions only at rare intervals. 5. Keep fixed daily lessons (the Rebbe names Chumash, Tehillim, Tanya) and do good actively where you are now.

**Step off the race with the neighbours** 1. Check whether a want is a need or a wish to equal or overtake someone nearby. 2. In material matters look downward, at those who have less, until you can be content with your lot. 3. Give up the wish to compete with the neighbours, and work the hours your real needs require. 4. Fill the freed time with learning, kindness to others and raising your children. 5. In spiritual matters do the opposite: look upward and keep the thirst.

## Reframes

- Envy is a disgrace I must suppress. → Envy is fuel. Aimed at another's good qualities it grows wisdom; left pointed at the person, it rots. *(Change what the feeling is about: from the person, to the good they have, which you can build in yourself.)* - My children, or my life, are better than other people's, so I'm fine. → In what matters, look up to those higher and grow; look down only in material things. *(The direction of comparison is reversed by domain: upward for growth, downward for possessions.)* - If I get one like his, I'm only even; I need two. → Competing is a race with no finish line, and it costs you your time with your children and your soul's purpose. *(Show the endlessness and the cost, then name the race as foreign to who you are.)* - Competition and ambition are bad; I should kill them. → Rivalry drives effort. Don't abolish it; raise it to a higher plane. *(Keep the drive and change the field it runs in.)* - I have to measure up on the same scale as everyone else. → People differ like faces. Fulfilment comes in the field where you are distinctive. *(Replace the common scoreboard with your own field.)* - My criticism of him is just fair judgement. → Belittling another's good deed may be envy that cannot bear his virtue. *(Recognise envy that has disguised itself as critique.)* - The great ones have everything; only I lack. → A scholar envied a simple labourer's plain devotion, and a greater scholar envied that scholar. It is the soul's longing, not a scoreboard. *(Everyone lacks something another has; the pull is a longing to be prayed for, not a verdict on you.)*

## Counsel in worldly matters

The letters send no one with envy to a doctor or professional. The counsel is practical: work only the hours real needs require; spend freed time on learning, kindness and children; cooperation in groups reduces envy; decide a match on its foundation, not on outsiders' view; keep fixed daily study. In one talk the Rebbe names nervous breakdowns and mental troubles as fruits of the race, but his remedy there is leaving the race, not referral.

## Where the Rebbeim differ

The Rebbe splits envy by object. Toward spiritual goods, increase it and turn it into effort. Toward material goods, reverse the gaze downward. The Rayatz gives a different entry: humility and compassion, since everyone has a native flaw and the other has hidden pains. The Alter Rebbe's letters speak of envy only as opponents' persecution, not as personal counsel. One talk adds a limit: holy envy must never take away a friend's honour.

## Cautions

- The 'increase it into effort' counsel can become a whip for someone depressed, perfectionistic or already overdriven. Don't use it there. - 'Look at those below you' can harden into contempt or smugness. The letters limit it to material things, and it must not be used to silence real deprivation or injustice. - The two-eyes teaching (see others' virtues, one's own faults) is not for someone already full of self-hatred. - When comparison concerns the body, eating or looks and is obsessive, or when envy turns into wish or intent to harm someone, the guide should send the person to a qualified professional. That comes from the guide's own duty; the letters do not say it. - Do not use holy envy to justify rivalry that takes away the other person's honour.

## Candidate truths

**Envy points; it does not own** (`envy_points_the_way`, fidelity: partly)

The sting of envy shows you what you long for. Its only healthy use is as fuel for your own next step toward that good. Aimed at the other person or at their having it, it rots you from inside.

> Critic: Q02/Q04: holy (scholars') envy increases wisdom, otherwise it stays base. Q24: envy is an inborn trait. Q14 (74555) says holy envy rots the animal soul's traits, a good thing, not that envy rots you. Also the passages limit good envy to wisdom and mitzvos. Fix: read Q14 correctly and narrow the scope.

**Up in what matters, down in what you own** (`look_up_look_down`, fidelity: yes)

Comparison has a right direction. In growth, character and meaning, look at those ahead and strive. In possessions and comforts, look at those with less, and you become content. Reversed, it makes you both complacent and miserable.

> Critic: Q06: in spiritual matters look up, in material matters look down. Q05: comforting yourself that your children are better than others. Q07: outward beauty is secondary. Q15/Q16: the antique-chair race has no end. Q20: content in earthly things, thirsting in spiritual ones. Faithful.

**Your own ground, not their scoreboard** (`your_own_ground`, fidelity: yes)

People differ in mind as they do in face. You are fulfilled by bringing out fully what is in you, in the field where you are distinctive. Racing someone else is foreign work with no end, and they carry pains you cannot see.

> Critic: Q12 (context 108654: minds differ as faces do): fulfilment comes from actualising your own distinctive field. Q13: channel competition into a higher field. Q29/Q30/Q18: racing neighbours is foreign work and leaves no time for purpose. Q25: others have private pains. Faithful.

It comes only from the soul's longing, the soul being drawn to G-dliness.</p> <p class="he">אם רק יוותר על הרצון להתחרות עם השכנים ולהשיג אותם – אזי יספיק לו לעסוק בפרנסה במשך שעות אחדות ביום, או במשך ימים אחדים בשבוע, בלא לב ולב, וללא פיזור הנפש</p> <p class="ref">B18:Q29 · Toras Menachem (the Lubavitcher Rebbe's talks, 1950-1992), Vol. 36 (5723, part 2), section 24 (line 65751)</p> <p class="en">If only he gives up the desire to compete with the neighbours and catch up with them, it will suffice to work for a living a few hours a day, or a few days a week, wholeheartedly and without scattering of the soul.</p> <p class="he">עבודה שהיא זרה לך " – אין זה ענינך לאסוף כסף וזהב ולרדוף אחרי השכן, להתחרות עמו ולהשיגו</p> <p class="ref">B18:Q30 · Toras Menachem (the Lubavitcher Rebbe's talks, 1950-1992), Vol. 36 (5723, part 2), section 24 (line 65752)</p> <p class="en">'A service that is foreign to you': it is not your business to gather silver and gold and chase after the neighbour, to compete with him and overtake him.</p> <p class="he">וחיו חיים מלאים ושלמים, חיים שיש בהם תוכן, והיו מאושרים, ולא היו אצלם כל הצרות של "התמוטטות עצבים"</p> <p class="ref">B18:Q31 · Toras Menachem (the Lubavitcher Rebbe's talks, 1950-1992), Vol. 36 (5723, part 2), section 24 (line 65749)</p> <p class="en">[His father and grandfather had only a limited number of dollars] and lived full and whole lives, lives with content, and were happy, and did not have all the troubles of 'nervous breakdowns'.</p>

## Gaps

I found no letter where someone envies a sibling, a spouse or a friend's marriage, children or health. None deals with envy of looks or bodies, or with social-media-style constant comparison, and none with envy that has turned bitter or hostile. The letters give no graded, step-by-step practice for the feeling itself and never mention a professional. Maamarei Admur HaZaken was not searched. The Rebbe's many 'shameach bechelko' letters to people who are ill or discouraged were left out as off-topic.

## B19 · Getting old, retiring, and feeling useless

*I stopped working and now the days are empty. I did my part, so who needs me now? My memory is going and I feel weak. The children put me somewhere comfortable, I'm treated with respect, and nobody asks what I think.*

**Coverage.** Probes counted in the Lubavitcher Rebbe's letters and talks, the Rayatz's letters, and Sefer HaSichos Rayatz: planned probes (gil shiv'im, yemei hazikna, ziknei anash, mosif v'holech) plus my own: pensia/pensie, gimla'os, retire (in Yiddish spelling), zikna, l'eis zikna, moshav zkeinim, zmanam panui, mishnayos b'al peh, yenuvun b'seiva, ben shiv'im l'seiva. The richest probes were gimla'os, retire, moshav zkeinim and mishnayos b'al peh. I read about 12 letters closely and about 8 talk passages, including the 11 Nissan 5732 seventieth-birthday talk. The Rayatz files turned up only incidental uses of the word "old". About 30 calls in all.

**What the sources do.** thin. The Lubavitcher Rebbe answers it directly in a handful of letters and several pointed talks; the Rayatz collections searched are silent apart from his instruction, relayed by the Rebbe, to learn Mishnah by heart in old age.

## The reading

The Lubavitcher Rebbe does not treat age as a number. He reads the Baal Shem Tov's "do not cast me off in old age" as a prayer not to be thrown into feeling old. Someone is old when their service has gone stale, and a young person can be old in this sense too. He treats retirement as freed time. It removes the excuse that business came first, so the retiree has more reason to learn and to act. At his own seventieth birthday he was asked whether it was time to rest. He answered that this is when to start with added strength, and that someone who feels he has "done it all" should want twice as much. He sets two things against despair. Wisdom gained over a life works through the whole person and is not left on a shelf. What was learned earlier still bears fruit when there is no strength left to plough. Where you end up living, even an old-age home, is where Providence has placed you to give. For weakness and forgetfulness his letters give a gentle routine. He also exempts people whose limits stop them acting with their own hands; they act through others.

## Real cases from the letters

- **Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1954).** *A woman abroad becomes entitled at seventy to a monthly state pension and is unsure whether she should accept it.* — Everything is by Providence. A chance to enlarge one's share in good things should be taken as a hint from above. Take the pension and use it well. If it is not needed for living, it can go to charity, especially the education of children.
- **Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1957).** *An older man writes about the state of his health.* — A blessing for better health and long life, and the Rayatz's known instruction: in old age, learn Mishnah by heart. Keep the three daily portions (Chumash, Psalms, Tanya) and say the psalm matching your age.
- **Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1959).** *A man explains his long silence by saying he has nothing to do (the letter does not give his age).* — Astonishment. Thousands are thirsting for the word of God, and he, who was given this knowledge, writes that he has nothing to do. From now on let him reflect and work at spreading it.
- **Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1971).** *A man writes after a long gap that he has retired, and that he did not leave public work but was elected head of a regional council.* — Since he has retired, his time is more his own, so he can surely do more. The Rebbe is especially glad that he did not withdraw from public work and an active role, and blesses him in the new post.
- **Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1955).** *A man writes that he feels weakness and forgetfulness.* — A blessing. Keep the three daily portions and say the psalm for your age. Learn Mishnah, and some of it by heart. When one subject tires you, switch to another that your heart wants. Adding heartfelt prayer lifts you above forgetting and strengthens memory.
- **Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1956).** *A man of advanced age asks for a blessing and mentions that he lives among people who have free time.* — Have your tefillin checked, keep the three daily portions, and suited to your age set fixed Mishnah study, some of it by heart. Living among people with free time, see yourself as an emissary: share Jewish practice and the laws of daily life.
- **Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1955).** *A man writes that he and his wife have moved into an old-age home.* — Everything is by Providence and can serve God. Providence brought him to this home so that he would add light there, and he will find pleasant, peaceable ways to do it. If he improves his neighbours' spiritual health, God will add to the couple's own health.
- **Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1950).** *An elder turns seventy.* — Blessed that in old age he will still bear fruit, 'fresh and flourishing', and so be occupied in telling and drawing down that God is upright.

## False conclusions and their corrections

- **“I've done my part. Now it's time to rest.”** — Asked at seventy whether it was time to think about rest, the Rebbe answered that it is not a time to rest and go travelling because one has already done it all. It is the time to begin with added strength, and someone who has a hundred should want two hundred.
- **“I'm old. People should honour me, not expect effort from me.”** — The talks name exactly this view: an old man need not toil, should sit up front and be honoured. The 'novelty' is that whatever his years, he must work hard at learning. Retirement is the very reason, because the old excuse of being busy is gone.
- **“I have nothing to do.”** — The letter answers with astonishment. Others are thirsting and he holds something to give, so he should begin now.
- **“I'm old because of my age.”** — Age depends on the person's state, not on the passport. A young person whose longing has gone stale is old, and the prayer is not to be cast into feeling old.
- **“My mind is going. What I learned is lost and I can't take in anything new.”** — What was learned earlier bears fruit when there is no strength to plough. Even in old age there is a part of a person that stayed untouched. The letters give a gentle way to keep learning despite forgetfulness.
- **“Being moved to a home means I've been put aside.”** — Providence brought you there to add light among your neighbours, and in return your own health is added to.

## Programs

**A daily anchor for the older years (from the letters)** 1. Keep three short daily portions: a section of Chumash, of Psalms, and of Tanya. 2. Each day say the psalm whose number matches your age (counting from bar mitzvah at chapter 14). 3. Set fixed times to learn Mishnah, and memorise some of it, a few passages at a time. 4. When one subject tires you, switch to another that your heart wants instead of pushing on. 5. Add heartfelt prayer, which the letter says lifts one above forgetting and strengthens memory.

**Treat a new home as a posting** 1. Take the move (old-age home, retirement community) as placed by Providence, not as being set aside. 2. Look for pleasant, peaceable ways to add light there. 3. Among neighbours with free time, see yourself as an emissary: share practice, learning, and the everyday laws. 4. Expect that caring for others' wellbeing adds to your own.

**Retirement as time given back** 1. Accept what is yours by right (a pension) and use the surplus for good, such as charity. 2. Do not retire from communal work or an active role; take on responsibility if it is offered. 3. Put the freed hours into what earning a living used to crowd out: fixed learning and deeper prayer.

## Reframes

- A pension is charity I'm embarrassed to take. → A hint from above that one's share in good can grow. *(Providence: whatever reaches you is for use, so take it and turn the surplus to good.)* - Retirement is the end of my usefulness. → My time is more my own than it has ever been. *(The removed obligation becomes freed capacity, and the old excuse for doing little is gone.)* - I'm old; the number says so. → Old means stale. If things are still new to me, I am not old. *(Age is measured by one's inner state and by whether the days are 'full', not by a birth certificate.)* - I'm too weak to do anything. → I can act through others who take up what I ask. *(The Rebbe explicitly allows for people whose limits keep them from acting with their own hands and feet.)* - My years of learning are just stored away. → The wisdom I gained works through my whole being and still bears fruit. *('An elder is one who acquired wisdom': it is not left on a shelf but changes the person, and it yields fruit when no new ploughing is possible.)* - They've put me in a home. → I've been posted somewhere to give. *(Every placement is by Providence and for service, and giving to neighbours returns as health.)*

## Counsel in worldly matters

The worldly advice is practical. Accept the state pension you are entitled to; this is plainly advised. Stay in, or take on, communal and public office. Engage with the neighbours in a new residence. Treat the freed hours as real work time. For weakness and forgetfulness these letters give a blessing, advice on how to learn, and, in one letter, checking tefillin. None of the aging letters found sends the person to a doctor. The guide must still recommend a medical assessment for memory loss or new weakness.

## Where the Rebbeim differ

The letters are gentle and personal: a small daily routine, switching subjects when tired, finding pleasant ways. The talks make demands: retirees are more obligated to toil, and at seventy one begins with added strength. Even the talks exempt people whose limits keep them from acting themselves. The Rayatz appears only through the Rebbe's report of his instruction to learn Mishnah by heart in old age; his own collections here say nothing on the subject.

## Cautions

- The 'do more, toil more' line from the talks was said to crowds, including at the Rebbe's own birthday. Do not use it on someone frail, ill, grieving or depressed; the Rebbe himself exempted people whose limits stop them acting themselves. - New forgetfulness or weakness needs a doctor. The letters found give only learning advice and blessings for it; the guide must send the person for medical assessment. - Loneliness or low mood after retirement can be clinical depression. If it persists or deepens, send the person to a professional, and if there are any thoughts of not wanting to live, to a person at once. - The rebuke in 'you write that you have nothing to do!' was addressed to someone able to give. Do not use it as shaming language toward a person who feels useless. - The routines (three daily portions, the psalm for one's age, Mishnah by heart) are Jewish practices. For others, translate the principle (a fixed daily anchor, memorising something meaningful, giving to those near you) rather than prescribing the rite. - Do not use 'Providence placed you in the home' to cover for neglect or mistreatment in a care setting.

## Candidate truths

**Old means stale, not the number of years** (`age_is_aliveness`, fidelity: partly)

How old you are depends on whether life is still new to you, not on the date on your papers. The prayer is not to be thrown into feeling old, and it can be answered at any age.

> Critic: Q21: 'do not cast me off' means not to feel old. Q22: it depends on your state, not your birth certificate. Q23: you are old if your longing in serving God has gone stale. Q28. The candidate makes it 'whether life is still new to you'. Fix: name the freshness of longing in serving God (secular version at most as a clothing).

**Freed time is given, not taken away** (`freed_time_is_a_charge`, fidelity: yes)

When work ends, the hours it used to fill come back to you, and the old reason for doing less is gone. Wherever you now find yourself, there is someone near you who needs what you have.

> Critic: Q06/Q19/Q20/Q25/Q27: retirement is a reason to do more, not rest. Q15: providence brought him to the old-age home to spread light there. Q26: those who can't act themselves act through others. Q01: take the pension and give charity with it. The passages mean more Torah and public work; say so.

**What you gathered still bears fruit** (`fruit_of_what_was_gathered`, fidelity: yes)

A life's learning and experience is not stored away; it has shaped who you are, and it keeps bearing fruit even when there is no strength left to plough.

> Critic: Q29: in old age, with no strength to plough, fruit from earlier learning. Q30: acquired wisdom is not left on the shelf. Both are about Torah learning, not general 'experience'. Q10 is prayer lifting memory above forgetting, a different claim from the 'anxious' door. Fix: tighten both.
