## Gaps

No letter found treats loneliness, loss of identity or low mood after retirement as a condition in itself. There is nothing on dementia, caring for an aging parent, or money fears in old age. There is no doctor referral for forgetfulness. The Igros here end in 1972, so the Rebbe's later letters on retirement are not in the library. The talks speak to crowds, mostly men; only one letter is to a woman. The Rayatz files are silent.

## B20 · Trying to help others and it isn't working

*I've poured years into these kids, this class, this person, and nothing changed. They drifted anyway. Maybe I'm no good at this. Maybe I should stop, or leave. What's the point if nobody's listening?*

**Coverage.** Rebbe's Igros Kodesh: counted 30+ probes. Planned probes were thin (1-11 hits each). The rich ones were נפילת רוח (56; all 56 skimmed), חוזרת ריקם (94; all skimmed), and אינו רואה/רואה פרי/בבקר זרע. Read closely: the 5717 letter to a youth worker (47500-12), plus 45044-5, 45444, 51679-80, 62036, 42690, 40693, 84440, 64062, 60993-4, 73061, 85955-6, 89997-90002, 90689, 32091, 65201. Rayatz Igros: 5 hits for חוזרת ריקם and 15 for יאוש; read 1718 and 1919. Toras Menachem: 8 hits for חוזרת ריקם; read 10811-19, 52700, 44084. About 60k characters read in all.

**What the sources do.** treats it. The plan expected this to be thin, but it is one of the Rebbe's most frequent themes: dozens of letters answer teachers, activists and rabbis who write that they see no results.

## The reading

The Rebbe (Igros Kodesh) says two things are being confused. One is the helper's task, which is to do their part. The other is the effect on the person being helped, which is 'a separate matter' and often hidden or late. It can appear only years later, when the person settles into family life. He cites his father-in-law's saying that sincere outreach never returns empty. He treats discouragement as more harmful than complacency, because the slackness it causes is greater. It is also the evil inclination's tactic: it shrinks in your eyes the results you did have. With an exhausted teacher he looks first for plain causes: tiredness, extra load, and a class too hard for a beginner. He also insists on honesty: if nothing lands at all, the method needs review. The Rebbe Rayatz calls despair in the face of obstacles 'an excuse, closer to falsehood', and says the little you can do is a lot.

## Real cases from the letters

- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (4 Tishrei 5717).** *A youth worker writes, low in spirit: years of work with young people, and they were swept into secular life anyway. He sees no fruit.* — Despair does more harm than complacency. Results often come late, sometimes only when the person settles into family life. Rabbi Akiva lost 24,000 students and rebuilt with five. Feel the bitterness, but the conclusion is to add effort and improve the work. Don't give up even on the weakest student, and don't compromise the truth, but move step by step. Words from the heart enter the heart.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1 Tammuz 5715).** *Someone gives a Torah class in a synagogue and feels low because the listeners show no change.* — It only seems that way. Torah must have an effect, but not always quickly, and people differ. Doing your part and the effect on the listener are two separate matters. Keep going and you will see fruit.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (11 Tammuz 5715).** *A man has been working to influence a young man's family and it seems to bear no fruit.* — Don't lose heart. Drops of water, one after another, carve even stone, and a Jewish heart is only called stone. It may take a little longer.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (26 Adar 5717).** *An activist adds a note that he sees no results from his work.* — That cannot be. The results could be bigger, but they come little by little. The inclination shrinks and hides the results you do have so that you will weaken. So add to the work: one action is better than a thousand sighs.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (12 Teves 5717).** *A rabbi describes the sad state of religious education in his community and thinks of leaving, since there is no one there to talk to.* — A bad situation isn't fixed by fleeing the battlefield. Staying and adding vigorous work can stop the decline and even improve things. Leaving carries responsibility.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1 Nissan 5717).** *A young man gathered friends to learn Torah together but is unhappy with how some of them behave.* — Consult an experienced mentor who knows them about the easiest way to influence them. It is forbidden to despair: try again and again, even a hundred times. Improving your own conduct will increase your influence.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (1 Shevat 5721).** *A young teacher, low after problems in her class, has left teaching for now and asks whether to return.* — There are three causes. She is very tired from the work plus other physical strain. The class was harder than a beginner should get. And the inclination seizes on fatigue to bring sadness. First decide not to listen to it, then take an easier class, then return only after real rest. This is an ordinary situation where several causes came together. Consult friends who know her and the situation, including the principal.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (25 Adar 5718).** *An educator, told that one of his actions was questionable, immediately concludes that all his work is worthless.* — Don't hide your mistakes, and don't fall in spirit either. Mistakes you overcome give light for the future. Where a rope tore and was knotted, it is doubled.

## False conclusions and their corrections

- **“I've had no effect on them at all.”** — The effect is often hidden or delayed. Sometimes the person hides it on purpose so as not to be pulled further, and it may show only years later. Even someone who thinks he achieved nothing did achieve something, without knowing it.
- **“Most of them were lost, so all my years were wasted.”** — A few who take it in fully can make up for all the rest, as Rabbi Akiva rebuilt with five students.
- **“Since I'm not succeeding, why should I care anymore?”** — That thought is a known tactic of the inclination. It shrinks the results you did have so that you weaken. Put the unfair accounting aside and keep working.
- **“One thing I did was criticized, so everything I do must be wrong.”** — Don't turn one criticism into a verdict on everything. Admit the mistake; a rope knotted where it tore is doubled there.
- **“There are too many obstacles; it's hopeless.”** — Despair in the face of obstacles is easy and serves as an excuse. It is closer to falsehood than to truth.
- **“Who am I? What difference can my little bit make?”** — The little is a lot. One seed yields many measures of fruit, and one child can grow into a great tree.

## Programs

**The worn-out helper: rest, lighten, then return** 1. Name the causes plainly. Ask whether you are physically exhausted from the work plus other strain, and whether you took on a harder assignment than fits where you are. 2. Decide not to listen to the inner voice that turns tiredness into sadness and giving up. 3. Choose an easier assignment for now (in the letter: the easiest class for discipline and level). 4. Go back only after real rest, so the body is not tired or weary. For some that is a few hours, for others several days. 5. See the situation as ordinary: several small causes came together at once. Each can be removed on its own. 6. Because a person is too close to himself to judge his own strength, consult friends who know you and the situation, including the person in charge.

**No fruit yet: keep sowing, and review the method** 1. Let yourself feel the pain over those who drifted, but draw one conclusion from it: add to the work and improve it. 2. Separate your task from the outcome. Your part is the doing; the effect on the other person is a separate matter. 3. Expect delay. Results often show only after a long time. 4. Don't give up on even the weakest person, and meet each at their pace, step by step, without pretending the partial is the whole. 5. Ask someone experienced who knows these people how to reach them more easily. Adjust your approach to each person's nature so they see you are not trying to erase who they are. 6. Speak from the heart, not from duty or obedience alone. 7. Try again and again, even a hundred times, and work on your own conduct, since it raises your influence. 8. If truly nothing is landing, treat that as a sign the method needs work, not that the effort is futile. 9. Replace sighing with one more concrete action.

## Reframes

- It isn't working → I did my part; whether and when it lands is a separate matter *(It separates the task from the outcome, so the worth of the work no longer depends on visible return)* - Nothing has changed → Drops wear the stone; the change is accumulating out of sight *(It replaces a verdict with a timescale; influence builds up and surfaces late)* - I failed with most of them → The few who take it in can make up for all *(It measures success by depth in a few people rather than numbers)* - My discouragement is an honest reading of the facts → Discouragement distorts the reading: it shrinks what did happen *(It exposes the discouraging voice as biased, so its accounting can be set aside)* - I should leave; it's hopeless here → Fleeing doesn't fix it; one action is worth a thousand sighs *(It turns grief into a single next deed)* - I'm just too tired and bad at this → Several ordinary causes came together; remove them one by one *(It breaks one overwhelming state into small practical causes)*

## Counsel in worldly matters

These letters send the person to people, not doctors. The discouraged teacher is told to consult friends who know her and the situation, and the school principal, because 'a person is too close to himself' to judge his own strength. The young man is sent to an experienced mentor who knows the people he wants to reach. Practical steps: real rest first, a lighter or easier assignment, preparing people earlier, and fitting the approach to each person. No letter read here refers this condition to a doctor.

## Where the Rebbeim differ

The Rebbe Rayatz is sterner: despair in the face of obstacles is an excuse, and everyone is personally responsible. The Rebbe adds psychology (delayed results, the inclination shrinking results) and practical load-reduction. Within the Rebbe's writing there is a deliberate tension. On one side: results don't matter to the task, just do your part. On the other: if nothing lands, that proves there wasn't real outreach, so review the method.

## Cautions

- Do not use 'your effort surely had an effect' to silence a helper who is exhausted or overloaded. For that person the Rebbe first prescribed rest, a lighter load, and consulting friends. - Do not use 'never give up' to justify pressure that is driving the other person away. The Rebbe says how hard to press depends on that person's nature. - The 'it's the inclination's tactic' framing is for ordinary discouragement. If low mood is persistent, deep or spreading beyond the work, it needs a person or professional. These letters do not cover that. - These letters do not address situations where helping someone is harming the helper (abuse, danger, addiction in the home). Do not apply 'keep going' there. - Don't let 'the effect is separate from me' become a way to avoid honest review. The Rebbe also says that where nothing lands, the approach must be examined.

## Candidate truths

**Your part and its effect are two different things** (`your_part_and_its_effect`, fidelity: partly)

What is asked of you is the doing. Whether and when it takes hold in another person is a separate matter, often hidden and often late, and it does not decide whether your work counted.

> Critic: Q08/Q30: your duty and its effect on others are separate, and doing is the main thing. Q07: it only seems to have no effect. But Q08 (45045) and Q13 (42690) hold that outreach never comes back empty, and no effect proves there was no real outreach. Fix: add that assurance, and keep Q13 only as a method check.

**Influence ripens late** (`late_ripening`, fidelity: yes)

Influence builds like water on stone, drop by drop and unseen. It often surfaces only years later, and a few who truly take it in can outweigh the many who didn't.

> Critic: Q09: water on stone, drop by drop. Q03: results often show only later, even after marriage. Q04: great success with a few fills what was lacking with the many. Q28: results are often not seen. Faithful.

**Don't judge your work while you're depleted** (`dont_judge_while_depleted`, fidelity: yes)

Discouragement is heaviest when you are tired and overloaded, and it misreads the facts: it shrinks what did happen and turns one mistake into a verdict on everything. Rest, lighten the load, and ask people who know you before you decide.

> Critic: Q24-Q26: a tired teacher, several causes at once, nothing unusual, so take the easier class and rest first. Q27: consult friends. Q10: the evil inclination shrinks real results. Q22/Q23: one criticism becomes a rule for everything, but a knotted rope is doubled. Faithful.

## Gaps

Nearly all these letters are about religious outreach and education. Helping a struggling relative (an addicted family member, a drifting adult child, a depressed spouse) is not covered by what I read. Nothing here says when to stop helping someone who refuses, or protects a helper who is being harmed. Burnout gets practical treatment in only one letter. No doctor or therapist referral was found for this condition.

## B21 · Surviving persecution, war and being uprooted

*I lived through it and others didn't. The pictures come back without asking and I get agitated for no reason. I'm in a new country and every setback feels like the end. I can't make sense of what happened, and part of me is furious at God.*

**Coverage.** Igros Kodesh (Rebbe): counted and grepped השואה, שארית הפליטה, ניצולי, מחנות, פליטים, איבערלעבונגען, מה שעבר עליו, טלטולים, אוד מוצל, היטלער (84 plus 71 hits). Closely read about 14 letters (1952-72). Igros Rayatz: הפליטים/ניצלו/מחנה הפליטים (32 hits). These are almost all rescue logistics, with 1 line kept. Sefer HaSichos Rayatz: nothing on survivors. Hayom Yom: nothing. Toras Menachem: ששה מליון/היטלער (21 hits); read the 5730 and 5731 talks. The planned probes אחר השואה (1 hit) and מאסר וגאולה (0) found nothing. About 26 tool calls.

**What the sources do.** treats it: the Rebbe's letters of 1952-72 answer survivors and refugees directly, both on their worries in daily life and on the question of faith. The Rayatz letters are about organising rescue, not about inner counsel.

## The reading

For the Rebbe, surviving is two things at once: a living miracle, and a task. A survivor's present despair, whether over money, a new country or fear of the next disaster, contradicts the rescue they themselves describe. The fear of "what if something bitter happens" is itself the bitter thing. He calls the pain real and not imagined. He calls the question "how could the Judge of the earth do this" authentic when it comes from the broken heart of a believer. He gives no answer and says so. He refuses to say it was deserved, and he refuses to say it was "worth it". Remembering is a mitzvah, but living inside the memory feeds pessimism and drains the energy needed to build. So the first task is "you shall live": a daily routine, marriage, a home, children, helping others. That is also the real defeat of the plan to destroy. For intrusive thoughts: follow the doctor, don't dwell, and hold a few memorised lines of Torah ready.

## Real cases from the letters

- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 1284, 5712).** *A young married woman, one of the survivor generation, wrote that she lived in fear that 'something bad might happen'.* — Trust is essential, especially now, since all of us, the remnant, are living miracles simply by existing. The very thought 'what if something bitter happens' is itself the bitter event. The strength it uses up is needed for building. Serve God with joy.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 1553, 6 Iyar 5712).** *A refugee recently arrived in a new country, with his wife unwell, wrote that he was heartbroken over earning a living.* — He and everyone around him saw wonders in the recent years; God saved the remnant miraculously. After that there is no room for a broken heart, especially so soon after arriving. The One who feeds all will provide. Later he will wonder why it seemed so dark. Serve with joy, until even the body understands.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 2857, 12 Av 5714).** *A man who had survived by 'real miracles' and built a family wrote that he was in debt and had lost his joy.* — The joyless end of his letter contradicts its opening. If God could save him then and let him build a home, all the more He can provide a living. It depends on trust and on charity. Say the daily portion of Psalms after morning prayers and study a verse of Chumash with Rashi each day.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 1861, 22 Elul 5712), in Yiddish.** *A man in the Holy Land wrote in Yiddish about what he had lived through: he was easily agitated and unwanted thoughts kept coming. The letter does not name the events.* — Take your mind off it. Of course do what the doctor says, but that is for action; brooding on it is not your business. Learn a few chapters of Mishnah and Tanya by heart, without straining. When the agitation or thoughts come, think a few lines of them. A little light pushes away much darkness, and this darkness is imagined.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 4444, 13 Sivan 5717), in Yiddish.** *A Russian-speaking émigré described his wanderings, ordeals and family misfortunes, and wrote that he could see no sense in them.* — A person sees only a limited part of what happens to him. Someone who walks into a surgery sees cutting and a person groaning; knowing the reason, he sees healing. The pain is real, not imagined, yet it is part of an order. Look back and see the direction. Strengthen trust, look kindly at the people around you, and light up your surroundings.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 2943, 22 Elul 5714).** *A woman educator who had come out of Russia described her state of mind and doubts in faith that were stopping her from teaching.* — Those rescued from the decrees were not saved for their own merit but for a purpose: from now on they must fill their role. She should use her gift for influence on the young even before every doubt is settled, because each day lost is a loss that does not return.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letters 8969, 5725; 9425, 5728; 10399, 5732; mostly Yiddish).** *A survivor, a writer, who publicly protested against God over the Holocaust, lived alone, and wrote much about the memory.* — The protest of a pained believer is authentic; Abraham and Moses asked it. After the storm comes 'nevertheless I believe'. Remembering is a mitzvah, but the first task is 'you shall live': tear yourself out of those experiences, marry, build a home. Dwelling breeds pessimism. He admits it is hard to ask this.
- **The Lubavitcher Rebbe, Igros Kodesh (letter 9323, 28 Iyar 5727).** *A woman wrote about the Holocaust and the overwhelming question of what it could mean.* — To human feeling it is utterly beyond the ordinary, and so is the question. But the question 'shall the Judge not do justice' is the same for one man's suffering as for millions. Rebellion on its basis has no footing. It is an old question. This is not an answer; the question comes from a broken heart, and no one is held to account for their grief.

## False conclusions and their corrections

- **“After what happened, faith is over; my rebellion is justified.”** — The question is as old as Moses and does not differ between one person's pain and millions. The deepest protesters stayed believers. The letters still admit that none of this is an answer.
- **“My situation now is hopeless; I have no reason for joy.”** — The same letter that despairs describes being saved by miracles and building a family. If that was possible, today's problem is smaller.
- **“To be faithful to the dead I must keep living inside the memory.”** — Remembering is a mitzvah, but dwelling feeds pessimism and drains the strength to build. The first task is to live.
- **“Worrying about the next catastrophe is only being careful.”** — The thought 'what if something bitter happens' is itself a bitter event, and it wastes strength needed for building.
- **“What happened to me is senseless, so the world is chaos.”** — A person sees only a small part of what happens to him. The pain is real, but it is not the whole picture.
- **“Only those who fought back died with honour; we who just endured did nothing.”** — Holiness was also shown by those who held on to daily Jewish life under inhuman conditions.

## Programs

**When the memories and agitation come** 1. Do what the doctor prescribes; that part is action and must be done. 2. Do not sit and analyse the experiences; take the mind off them deliberately. 3. In a calm time, learn a few chapters of Mishnah and of Tanya by heart, gently and without strain. 4. When agitation rises or unwanted thoughts come, go over a few memorised lines in the mind. 5. Put the freed time into personal affairs and into helping the community.

**Back into the order of life** 1. Keep remembering; do not suppress the memory, since remembering is a mitzvah. 2. Recognise that constantly reliving it feeds pessimism and saps energy. 3. Make every effort to step out of 'that moment' into a daily routine of life. 4. Build a home and family where possible, and build Jewish life around you. 5. Use your particular gifts to strengthen others.

**Answering today's worry from yesterday's rescue** 1. Recall concretely how you were brought through. 2. Reason from it: if that was possible, today's need is smaller. 3. Put your trust in the One who provides, and give charity. 4. Each day after morning prayers say the day's portion of Psalms and study a verse of Chumash with Rashi.

## Reframes

- I am a leftover who happened to survive. → Your existence is a living miracle, and you were kept for something. *(It turns survival from chance, or guilt, into a mission and a place in the world.)* - My anger at God means I have lost my faith. → The cry 'how could the Judge do this' is a believer's cry; Abraham and Moses asked it. *(The protest is taken as evidence of faith. After the storm comes 'nevertheless I believe'.)* - Honouring the past means staying in it. → Building a living family and community is the answer to those who wanted none to remain. *(Memory becomes a push to build rather than a place to live.)* - Those murdered were simply destroyed. → A bullet or a knife cannot touch the life of the soul. *(It separates the body's death from the soul's continuation. It does not explain why.)* - We who just endured showed no courage. → Keeping up daily Jewish life under inhuman conditions was itself a sanctification of God's name. *(It widens what counts as heroic to include quiet endurance.)* - The good that came afterwards makes it all worth it. → God forbid anyone should say it was worth it. *(It refuses a cheap theodicy and keeps the loss honest.)*

## Counsel in worldly matters

For agitation and intrusive thoughts the Rebbe writes: of course do what the doctor says. He frames medical care as action that must be done, alongside not dwelling on the events. For refugees he gives practical settling advice: where to live, work, a stable place and community; in one letter he weighs the United States against other countries for a person of a certain age. The Rayatz letters are about practical rescue: offices to help refugees, and lists of those in the camps so relatives could be traced. No letter found refers anyone to a psychologist.

## Where the Rebbeim differ

The Rayatz's parable, reported in a talk, reads the catastrophe as a corrective slap. The Rebbe says openly that he did not want to justify God or explain the suffering, and that 'we sinned' is no answer about God's conduct. In tone, the Rebbe's early-1950s letters are brisk ('I was astonished'). His later letters to survivor writers admit how hard it is to ask this, and that it is doubtful he should.

## Cautions

- Never tell a survivor it was punishment or that it was 'worth it'. The Rebbe himself refuses both. - The brisk 'I was surprised at your despair' tone was written by a Rebbe to people who knew him. A guide must not rebuke a survivor's grief or fear. - Do not load 'you were saved for a purpose' or 'you must replace the dead' onto someone sinking under guilt. It can crush rather than lift. - Do not press marriage or children on someone for whom that is not possible or not yet possible. - Intrusive memories, agitation, nightmares or panic are to be treated by a doctor or trauma professional first, as the letter itself says. Torah recitation goes alongside care, never instead of it. - If a survivor speaks of not wanting to live, this must go to a real person at once.

## Candidate truths

**Living is the answer** (`living_is_the_answer`, fidelity: yes)

Whoever meant to destroy you is answered by your life going on. Keep the memory, but build a daily life, a home and a circle of people, because that is what they tried to erase.

> Critic: Q12/Q13: first task, 'you live', visibly: an orderly life, marriage, a Jewish home. The context (109997) says outright to build a large family to spite Hitler. Q14: remembering is positive. Q15/Q16/Q18: memory weakens energy, and letting go is hard to demand. Fix: the source says family, not 'a circle of people'.

**The question of a broken heart** (`question_from_a_broken_heart`, fidelity: yes)

Crying out 'how could this be allowed' is not the opposite of faith. The deepest believers asked it. There is no answer, and a person can hold the question and still go on living and doing good.

> Critic: Q07/Q09: the question comes from a broken heart, and there is no answer. Q08: no one is held to account for his grief. Q10/Q11: the protest is real from a believer, ending in 'nevertheless I believe'. Q23: 'we sinned' is no answer about God. Q25: God forbid saying it was worth it. Q27. Faithful.

**What carried you then can carry you now** (`yesterday_carries_today`, fidelity: yes)

The fact that you were brought through the worst is evidence you can lean on when today's troubles seem overwhelming. Fear of the next disaster only uses up the strength you need now.

> Critic: Q01: survivors are living miracles, and fearing the next disaster is itself a bitter event. Q02/Q03: having seen wonders, there is no room for a broken heart. Q04: if God saved you, all the more He will provide a living. The passages are firmer ('no room') than the candidate's gentler tone. Faithful.

## Gaps

No letter found on survivors of later wars (wounded soldiers, bereaved soldiers, captivity trauma), or on non-Jewish or non-Holocaust uprooting. Nothing addresses survivor guilt as a feeling; the letters turn it into responsibility. Nightmares and panic are covered only by 'follow the doctor, don't dwell'. The Rayatz letters give rescue logistics, not inner counsel. Some cases, such as the Yiddish letter on agitation, do not name the events that were lived through.

## B22 · Thoughts of not wanting to live

*I don't want to be here anymore. Everyone would be better off without me. I've thought about how I would end it. I'm not sure I can keep going through tonight.*

**Coverage.** Counted in all 14 collections: מאבד עצמו לדעת, לאבד (את) עצמו, מאבד את עצמו, טוב מותי, קצתי/מאסתי בחיי. Hits only in the Rebbe's letters (2) and Toras Menachem (about 10, mostly 'losing oneself' in a figurative sense). None in the Rayatz, Rashab, Tzemach Tzedek, Mitteler or Alter Rebbe material, or Hayom Yom. Also counted in the letters: התאבדות, רוצה למות, לא לחיות, אין טעם לחיים, פסיכיאטר, דכאון, חולי/מחלת נפש, להתייאש, ייאוש. Read every suicide hit in context, plus the depression and mental-illness hits. About 9 calls.

**What the sources do.** silent: no letter or talk in this library answers a person who wrote that they wanted to die. Suicide comes up only as a legal prohibition, as a comparison in an argument about something else, and once, in marriage advice, as a threat by a third party.

## The reading

The Lubavitcher Rebbe never treats this condition directly in these texts. He mentions it only in passing, as something everyone agrees on: when someone means to take their own life, society uses every means to stop them. It does not wait for permission, even when the person is an adult and insists it is his own business. He adds that a person whose mind is settled could not want this, so he frames the wish as a sign of a mind that is not settled, not a free choice to respect. The talks also cite the legal ban in passing. None of this is pastoral counsel to someone at risk.

## Counsel in worldly matters

The one working principle in the texts is intervention. When a life is at risk, others use every means to stop it and do not ask the person's permission (Q01, Q02). On psychiatric illness, the Rebbe writes that even an hour's rescue is worth every effort (Q06). He calls psychiatry the doctor's own field and apologizes for writing into it (Q07). For the guide: send the person at once to a live human, whether emergency services, a crisis line, or a trusted person nearby, and to professional mental-health care. The texts give no spiritual program that could replace this.

## Where the Rebbeim differ

Only the Lubavitcher Rebbe mentions it at all. The earlier Rebbeim are silent in this library. Within his writings there is a tension. The talk says everyone must stop a person who wants to die (Q02). A marriage letter says a partner's threat of suicide does not oblige the other to stay in the relationship (Q04). That letter is about not giving in to a threat. It is not about how to care for the person making it.

## Cautions

- This must go to a person at once: emergency services, a crisis line, or a trusted person physically nearby. The guide must say so plainly and first, and must not attempt a Chassidus-based treatment in place of that. - Do not lead with or cite the prohibition on taking one's life (Q05) to someone at risk. In the text it is a legal aside, not counsel to a sufferer. - Do not use the 'there is no room for despair' letters (Q08 and similar) as a rebuke or as treatment here. They answer low mood in general, not suicidal thinking. - Do not use the marriage-advice line (Q04) to play down anyone's threat of self-harm. It concerns not giving in to pressure and says nothing about the danger itself. - Do not tell the person that a settled mind could not want this (Q03). In the text it is a premise in an argument, not something to say to someone suffering, and it can land as shaming.

Rather, one must keep a bond and an open channel with God, the source of life and blessing.</p>

## Gaps

No letter answers someone who says they want to die, and there is no crisis protocol, warning signs, or follow-up. The letters that answer despair or depression (for example lines 53788, 75199-75200, 119321) belong to other conditions and must not be moved over to this one. The Rebbe's English letters, which may hold relevant correspondence, are not in this Hebrew library. The safety floor has to be built entirely outside these texts.
